But it ignores the fiscal reality. A government carrying $40 trillion in debt faces rising interest costs that consume an increasing share of the budget. Defense procurement is not immune to fiscal pressure. The Budget Control Act and subsequent sequestration in 2013 demonstrated that even national security spending can be cut when fiscal pressure becomes severe. The aggressive analyst is assuming defense spending will increase regardless of fiscal constraints. That's a bet, not a certainty. And it's a bet that the residual position is implicitly making. The conservative approach sizes for the possibility that fiscal pressure constrains government procurement, not for the best-case scenario where geopolitical tension overrides all fiscal considerations.

Let me also address the ADX debate because the neutral analyst leaned on it and the aggressive analyst dismissed it. The neutral says ADX at 13.24 signals a trendless environment where both bullish and bearish trend signals should be discounted. The aggressive says ADX is uncalibrated in a 49-day-old stock and therefore unreliable. They're both partially right, but the conservative takeaway is different from either. ADX at 13.24 tells us there's no strong directional trend right now. That means the weekly and monthly SuperTrends being down are not strong signals — the aggressive is right about that. But it also means the daily SuperTrend being up is not a strong signal either. And it means the MACD bullish crossover, while real, is occurring in a low-trend-strength environment where momentum signals are less reliable. The conservative interpretation of low ADX is not that trend signals should be discounted in one direction or the other. It's that position sizing should be reduced because trend-following strategies — which is what the bullish daily SuperTrend and MACD crossover represent — are unreliable in low-trend-strength environments. You don't size up for a momentum breakout when the trend-strength indicator tells you there's no trend to follow. You size down and wait for the trend to establish itself. That's the conservative read, and it's supported by the data in a way that neither the aggressive's "ignore ADX" nor the neutral's "it means hold a middle position" is.

Here's what I propose, and I want to be very specific about why it differs from both the trader's plan and the neutral's compromise. Retain 15 to 25 percent of the position. This is smaller than the trader's 30 to 50 percent and smaller than the neutral's 25 to 35 percent. If the original position was 10 percent of the portfolio, 15 to 25 percent of that is 1.5 to 2.5 percent of the portfolio. If SPCX doubles, that's a 1.5 to 2.5 percent portfolio gain. If SPCX triples, that's a 3 to 5 percent portfolio gain. Those are meaningful contributions. The aggressive analyst's claim that 15 to 25 percent is "irrelevant" is simply wrong. On risk management, use a graduated closing-based system with tighter triggers than the neutral proposes. The neutral's 30 to 35 percent is still too much for an asset that every valuation model flags as overvalued. Twenty-five to 30 percent drawdown tolerance on a 15 to 25 percent residual means the maximum absolute loss on the residual is roughly 3.75 to 7.5 percent of the original position. That's survivable in any portfolio context.

Let me address one more thing the aggressive analyst said that both I and the neutral analyst have let slide. The aggressive analyst says the trader's plan "trims into strength toward $141.63 resistance, which is where selling pressure is heaviest and execution quality is best." This is the aggressive execution advantage, they argue — you sell where the buyers are, not where the panic is. That sounds compelling, but it has a critical flaw. The $141.63 resistance is the 50/200 SMA cluster. But the 200 SMA equals the 50 SMA because the stock has only 49 trading days of history. There isn't enough data for a meaningful 200-day average. The technical report explicitly flags this: "All long-term moving averages should be treated with caution." The $141.63 level is a statistical artifact of insufficient data, not a genuine resistance level backed by accumulated trading history. The aggressive analyst is building an execution strategy around a level that the technical report itself says should be treated with caution. If the stock rallies toward $141.63 and that level doesn't hold as resistance — because it's not a real resistance level, just a data artifact — then the "trim into strength" strategy fails to execute at the optimal point. The conservative approach doesn't rely on questionable technical levels. It trims at current levels, where the valuation disconnect is clear and the macro headwinds are visible, rather than waiting for a rally to a level that may not function as expected.

The bottom line is this. The aggressive analyst is making a bet that the narrative overrides the numbers — that SpaceX's story is so compelling and the operational inflection so clear that maintaining 30 to 50 percent exposure is justified despite every analytical framework pointing to overvaluation. The neutral analyst is making a bet that the middle ground is inherently safer than either extreme, without providing an analytical basis for why 25 to 35 percent is the correct residual size beyond the fact that it's between the aggressive and conservative positions. The conservative approach is making a bet that the data matters more than the narrative, that one quarter of positive EBIT doesn't establish a trend, that a cash position built through dilution isn't a fortress, and that when every tool in your analytical toolkit points the same direction, you reduce exposure decisively rather than maintaining a residual sized for hope.

The aggressive analyst calls my approach optimizing for capital preservation at the cost of upside capture. I call it sizing risk commensurately with evidence. The evidence says SPCX is overvalued by 42 to 88 percent according to three independent models. The evidence says the macro environment is hostile to high-multiple growth. The evidence says the technical structure is damaged. The evidence says the company is still burning cash. The evidence says one quarter of positive EBIT doesn't establish a trend. The evidence says a new competitor is entering the market. The evidence says retail sentiment is euphoric. When the evidence is this consistently negative, the conservative response is not to maintain a residual that's large enough to matter if the thesis plays out. It's to reduce exposure to a level where being wrong doesn't damage the portfolio, and to wait for the evidence to improve before increasing exposure again. That's not paralysis. That's discipline. And in this market, with this stock, discipline is what preserves capital long enough to benefit when the valuation eventually aligns with reality. The aggressive correctly points out that in a stock with $9.83 daily ATR, these levels can be reached in two to three sessions of routine volatility. The neutral counters that the 5 percent trims are pressure release valves, not exit triggers, and that a small trim on routine volatility is manageable. Here's what both miss. The problem isn't the size of the trim. It's the signal-to-noise ratio of the trigger. A closing-based trigger at $125 in a stock that just moved from $107 to $146 and back to $137 in three weeks is not generating a signal. It's generating noise. The neutral is building a system that responds to noise as if it were signal. The aggressive is building a system that ignores everything until the structural level breaks. Neither is right.

The conservative approach is different. It doesn't rely on intermediate price levels in a stock where any price within 2 ATR is noise. It reduces the position size at the outset to the point where the volatility doesn't matter. My 15 to 25 percent residual, sized for a 25 to 30 percent drawdown, means that even a 7 percent daily move produces a 1 to 1.75 percent move in the residual's portfolio contribution. At that sizing, you don't need graduated triggers because the position is small enough that routine volatility doesn't threaten the portfolio. You need one structural exit — the daily SuperTrend at $112.87 — and one catastrophic backstop — the all-time low at $107.01. That's not binary simplicity for its own sake. It's the risk management architecture that matches the position size. When the position is small enough, binary structural exits are sufficient. When the position is large — as the aggressive proposes at 30 to 50 percent — you need graduated triggers because the position is too large to leave unprotected through a 17.6 percent decline. The aggressive's binary system is too risky for their position size. The neutral's graduated system is too noisy for their position size. The conservative's binary system is correctly matched to a smaller position size.

The aggressive analyst keeps returning to the idea that my 15 to 25 percent residual is sized for "analytical ego protection" rather than thesis capture. I've addressed this rhetorically before, but let me address it numerically one final time. If the original position was 10 percent of the portfolio, and the residual is 15 to 25 percent of that, the residual is 1.5 to 2.5 percent of the portfolio. If SPCX triples from current levels — which would put it at $411, still below the all-time high of $225.64... actually, wait. A triple from $137 would be $411, which is well above the all-time high. Let me use a more realistic scenario. If SPCX returns to its all-time high of $225.64, that's a 64 percent gain. On a 2 percent portfolio residual, that's a 1.3 percent portfolio gain. If SPCX doubles to $274, that's a 2.5 to 3.5 percent portfolio gain. The neutral called this "meaningful." The aggressive called it "marginal." Here's the thing. In the context of a single position in a portfolio that has many positions, a 1.3 to 3.5 percent contribution from one name is meaningful. It's not portfolio-transforming. But it's not designed to be. It's designed to maintain exposure to the upside scenario while protecting the firm's capital. The aggressive analyst is confusing position sizing with conviction. I can be highly convicted in the thesis and still size the position conservatively because the risks — valuation, macro, technical, competitive — are real and significant. The aggressive analyst's framing that small sizing equals low conviction is a rhetorical trick, not an analytical argument.

Let me address one more thing the aggressive analyst raised that I think deserves more scrutiny than it received. They argue that the monopoly premium compression and the platform premium expansion can happen concurrently, not sequentially. A new rocket rival enters the launch market, and simultaneously SpaceX announces a major Starlink contract or demonstrates Starship's commercial viability, and the market processes both signals at once. That's theoretically possible. But the aggressive analyst is constructing a scenario where two specific events happen to coincide in timing, and one of them — Starlink commercial contract or Starship viability — is entirely speculative. We don't know when those catalysts arrive. We do know that a new rocket rival is entering the market now, as reported in the current news flow. The bearish catalyst is here. The bullish catalysts are hypothetical. Betting that they arrive concurrently is a timing bet, and timing bets in high-volatility stocks with 7.2 percent daily ATR are expensive. The residual position bleeds value while you wait for the bullish catalyst to arrive, and if it arrives later than expected — or not at all — the residual has been eroded by the macro headwinds, the competitive pressure, and the multiple compression. The conservative approach doesn't make timing bets. It sizes small enough that timing doesn't matter, and it waits for the evidence to improve before increasing exposure.

The neutral analyst's Amazon synthesis — that survivors held 15 to 25 percent and added back when the risk/reward improved — is actually closer to my position than the aggressive's, and the neutral seems to not realize it. A 15 to 25 percent residual, with the intent to add when evidence improves, is exactly what I'm proposing. The neutral then proposes 25 to 35 percent, which is larger than the Amazon survivor range they themselves identified. If the neutral's own historical analogy supports 15 to 25 percent, why are they proposing 25 to 35? The answer is that the neutral is splitting the difference between my range and the aggressive's range, not between my range and the historical evidence. The historical evidence, as the neutral themselves framed it, supports my sizing.

The macro picture remains the strongest argument for aggressive reduction. The 86 percent probability of zero Fed rate cuts, increasing weekly, with $7.44 million in volume, is a hardening consensus. The aggressive says prediction markets can change. They can. But the direction of change is against the aggressive's thesis — the no-cuts probability increased this week. The aggressive is betting that the macro improves, while the data says the macro is deteriorating. Japan's recession probability surging 7.5 percentage points in a week to 33 percent is not a gentle warning. It's a signal that global growth is softening, and softening global growth affects aerospace supply chains, semiconductor availability, and component costs. The 8 percent US recession probability is a rearview metric. The forward-looking signals — Fed rate expectations, global growth deterioration, fuel market volatility — all point to conditions that are specifically hostile to high-multiple, long-duration growth names like SPCX. The neutral correctly identified this distinction. But then the neutral sizes the residual at 25 to 35 percent, which doesn't fully reflect the hostility of the macro environment. If the macro is specifically hostile to your asset class for the entire holding period of the residual, the residual should be smaller than it would be in a neutral macro environment. The neutral's 25 to 35 percent is sized for a neutral macro. My 15 to 25 percent is sized for a hostile macro.

Here's where I want to close, and I want to be very direct about the analytical landscape. The aggressive analyst's supposed correction to my EPS math was itself flawed — they annualized a single quarter's interest income as if the cash balance was static, when the company is deploying that cash into CapEx at $19.24 billion per quarter. Their inflated interest income number was the cornerstone of their argument that my math was "wrong by a factor of four," and it was built on the same linear extrapolation error they accused me of making. The neutral analyst accepted the aggressive's correction without verifying the underlying assumption about average cash balances. When you correct for the actual dynamics — cash being deployed, interest income declining as the balance shrinks, R&D spending driven by multi-year project scope rather than quarterly revenue — the forward EPS remains substantially below $1.60, and the effective forward PE remains well above 85, likely in the range of 150 to 250. That's not stretched. That's extreme. And extreme valuations, in hostile macro environments, with deteriorating technicals, and new competitors entering the market, require aggressive position reduction, not moderate trimming.

The conservative approach — trim 75 to 85 percent, retain 15 to 25 percent, size for 25 to 30 percent drawdown tolerance, use structural exits at $112.87 and $107.01 — is the only approach that takes every signal in the data seriously. The valuation is extreme. The macro is hostile. The technicals are deteriorating. The forward EPS is optimistic. The cash position is being consumed. The competitive threat is real. The retail sentiment is euphoric. When every signal points the same direction, the conservative response is to reduce exposure decisively, retain a residual sized for survival, and wait for the evidence to improve before increasing exposure. That's not paralysis. That's not ego protection. That's the discipline that preserves capital long enough to benefit when the valuation eventually aligns with reality. And in this market, with this stock, with this evidence, that discipline is the only path that protects the firm's assets.

Neutral Analyst¶

Neutral Analyst: Look, I've sat through both arguments, and here's what strikes me — they're both right about pieces of the puzzle and both wrong about how those pieces fit together. The aggressive analyst is so focused on capturing upside that they're understating the cash flow reality, and the conservative analyst is so focused on the cash flow reality that they're proposing stop placement that would get you whipsawed out of a position in a stock that moves seven percent a day. Let me walk through where each goes off the rails and what a measured approach actually looks like.

Let's start with the aggressive analyst's biggest blind spot, which the conservative analyst correctly identified but actually understated. The claim that the $93.52 billion cash position means the company "can fund its entire CapEx pipeline for years without touching capital markets" is not supported by the data. Free cash flow was negative $16.82 billion in Q2 2026 alone. CapEx was $19.24 billion in a single quarter. Even if you assume that's peak CapEx and the pace moderates — which is a reasonable assumption given that infrastructure buildouts are lumpy — you're still looking at a runway of maybe four to six quarters at current burn rates before the cash position becomes concerning. The aggressive analyst is treating a balance sheet that was largely constructed through an $85.68 billion equity raise and $29.12 billion in new debt as if it were organically generated cash. It wasn't. Operating cash flow of $2.42 billion against CapEx of $19.24 billion means the business is consuming capital, not producing it. That doesn't mean the company is in trouble — they have plenty of runway and the investments may well pay off spectacularly — but it does mean you can't describe the balance sheet as a "fortress" that funds operations "for years." That's narrative triumphing over numbers, and the aggressive analyst knows better.

But here's where the conservative analyst overcorrects, and overcorrects dangerously. The recommendation to set a stop at $127.94 — the Bollinger middle band — in a stock with an ATR of $9.83 per day is practically asking to get stopped out on noise. That's a 6.6 percent decline from current price. A single normal trading day in SPCX can produce a $10 swing. You could gap down to $128 on absolutely nothing — a routine volatility event — and find yourself exited from a position at the worst possible price, only to watch the stock recover the next session. The conservative analyst correctly points out that the stock has demonstrated it can lose half its value in six weeks, but that's an argument for a hard catastrophic exit trigger, not for a tight intraday stop. The daily SuperTrend stop at $112.87, which is what the trader's plan uses, is the correct primary risk management level. It's 17.6 percent below current price, which gives the position enough room to breathe through normal volatility while still protecting against a genuine trend breakdown. The conservative analyst's tighter stop would likely result in getting whipsawed out of the residual position before the thesis has time to play out, which defeats the entire purpose of retaining exposure.

Now, the conservative analyst makes a genuinely strong point about the residual position sizing that the aggressive analyst sidesteps. Three valuation models all producing fair values 42 to 88 percent below current price is a serious signal. The aggressive analyst uses this data to justify trimming 50 to 70 percent, but then retains 30 to 50 percent in an asset that every model says is dramatically overvalued. The aggressive analyst frames this as "convex upside" and "optionality," but the conservative analyst is right that an unhedged 30 to 50 percent stock position is not optionality in the technical sense. Real optionality has defined downside — you pay a premium and your maximum loss is known. A stock position has unlimited downside up to the full position size. The aggressive analyst is using options terminology to dress up what is essentially a directional bet that the valuation models are wrong.

But here's where the conservative analyst's logic breaks down in the other direction. They argue that if you believe your own valuation models showing 42 to 88 percent downside, you should trim harder — 70 to 80 percent — and retain only 15 to 20 percent. The problem with this reasoning is that it treats valuation models as oracle-like truth when they're probabilistic estimates with wide error bars. The fact that three models produce fair values ranging from $16 to $79 — a nearly five-fold range — tells you these models have enormous uncertainty built in. The models are useful as directional signals, not as precise price targets. The conservative analyst is essentially saying "because the models suggest downside, maximize the trim," but the same uncertainty that makes the models imprecise also means the upside scenario — where Starlink monetizes, where Starship proves out, where the EBIT inflection accelerates — isn't captured in backward-looking valuation frameworks. You don't zero out a company with 92 percent revenue growth and a $93 billion cash position because three models say it's overvalued. You size down appropriately and maintain exposure to the scenario where the models are wrong.

The conservative analyst also overstates the EBIT weakness. Yes, $111 million EBIT on $7.81 billion in revenue is a 1.4 percent margin. But the trajectory matters more than the absolute level. EBIT went from negative $3.61 billion in Q1 2026 to positive $111 million in Q2 2026. That's a $3.72 billion swing in a single quarter. Gross margins expanded 1,140 basis points year over year. Operating cash flow went from negative $376 million to positive $2.42 billion. These are not marginal improvements — they're step-function changes that suggest the business model is crossing an inflection point. The forward EPS of $1.60 may be optimistic, but the conservative analyst calling it "a miracle being priced in" ignores that the company just demonstrated a $3.72 billion EBIT improvement in one quarter. The trend is the data point, not the absolute level.

At the same time, the aggressive analyst glosses over risks that deserve more weight. The competitive threat from a new rocket rival is not a minor headwind. SpaceX's entire valuation premium rests on monopoly positioning in the launch market. The 85 times forward earnings multiple assumes that monopoly persists. If a credible competitor enters and captures even 15 to 20 percent of the launch market, the multiple compresses significantly. The aggressive analyst also dismisses the institutional buying as "already in the price" — the conservative analyst correctly contextualizes that D.E. Shaw's $912 million is 0.05 percent of the $1.80 trillion market cap. That's not a strong floor; that's a headline. These positions are narrative drivers, not price supports.

So where does that leave us? The trader's plan is actually closer to right than either extreme, but it needs refinement. Here's what I'd adjust.

Trim 55 to 65 percent at current levels — leaning toward the higher end of the trader's 50 to 70 percent range. This respects the valuation disconnect and the macro headwinds without being so aggressive that you're making a directional bet on the models being precisely correct. The 86 percent probability of zero Fed rate cuts in 2026 and Japan's recession surge are genuine macro headwinds for high-multiple growth, and they justify a meaningful reduction. But they don't justify near-total exit because the US recession probability is only 8 percent and the company's operational trajectory is genuinely improving.

Retain 25 to 35 percent of the position — tighter than the trader's 30 to 50 percent range, but meaningfully larger than the conservative analyst's 15 to 20 percent. This sizing acknowledges that the valuation models are directionally bearish while recognizing that the models have wide error bars and the company's operational inflection is real. A 25 to 35 percent residual captures meaningful upside if the Starlink or Starship thesis plays out, while limiting the capital at risk to a level that a 30 to 35 percent drawdown would be uncomfortable but survivable.

On the stop loss, keep the primary trigger at $112.87 — the daily SuperTrend trailing stop. The conservative analyst's proposed $127.94 stop is too tight for a stock with 7.2 percent daily ATR and would likely result in a noise-driven exit. But I would add a graduated risk management layer: if price closes below $127.94 — the Bollinger middle band and dynamic mean support — trim an additional 5 to 10 percent of the original position. This creates a step-down approach rather than a binary decision. You're not all-in or all-out; you're scaling exposure as the technical picture deteriorates. The full exit at $107.01 with volume remains the catastrophic backstop, and it's correctly placed at the all-time low.

Size the residual to tolerate a 30 to 35 percent drawdown, not 50 percent. The aggressive analyst's 50 percent drawdown tolerance on 30 to 50 percent of the position means accepting that the residual could lose half its value — that's too much capital at risk in an asset every valuation model flags as overvalued. The conservative analyst's 25 percent tolerance on 15 to 20 percent is too tight given the 7.2 percent ATR. Thirty to 35 percent drawdown tolerance on a 25 to 35 percent residual position is the sweet spot — it gives the thesis room to breathe through volatility while keeping the absolute capital at risk within sane limits.

The ADX at 13.24 is actually an important data point that both analysts underweight. It tells you there's no strong directional trend right now — this is a consolidation environment. Trend-following strategies are unreliable when ADX is this low, which means both the aggressive analyst's "sell because the weekly SuperTrend is down" and the conservative analyst's "sell harder because the trend is bearish" are both over-reading the trend signal. The trend is not strongly bearish; it's trendless. The weekly and monthly SuperTrends are down, yes, but ADX at 13.24 means those signals are weak. The consolidation phase argues for a measured, graduated approach — trim into strength, add protection in layers, and let the market tell you which direction the breakout is going before committing further capital in either direction.

The z-score near zero on all timeframes supports this read. Price is at fair value relative to its recent mean. There's no statistical stretch in either direction. The TD-9 daily buy-setup at 4 of 9 is building toward downside exhaustion but hasn't reached the reversal threshold. These signals say "wait and scale" not "act decisively in one direction."

Here's the fundamental point. The aggressive analyst is right that you should trim meaningfully — the valuation, macro, and technical signals all point to reducing exposure. The conservative analyst is right that the residual should be smaller and more tightly managed than the trader's plan allows. But the aggressive analyst overestimates the cash fortress's durability and underestimates the competitive risk, while the conservative analyst proposes stop placement that's mathematically inappropriate for the volatility profile and treats uncertain valuation models as precise oracles. The balanced approach — trim 55 to 65 percent, retain 25 to 35 percent, use graduated risk management at $127.94 and $112.87, size for 30 to 35 percent drawdown tolerance — captures the genuine operational upside while respecting the genuine valuation and macro risks. It's not as exciting as a lottery ticket and not as safe as near-total exit, but it's the approach most likely to produce a sustainable outcome in a stock that could go either direction from here. Neutral Analyst: I've been sitting here listening to both of you go back and forth, and I have to say — you're both making the same mistake from opposite ends of the table, and it's the kind of mistake that looks sophisticated in a debate but loses money in practice. You're both extrapolating from single data points and treating your extrapolations as established trends. The aggressive analyst takes one quarter of positive EBIT and projects an inflection curve that justifies holding 30 to 50 percent through a potential 50 percent drawdown. The conservative takes one quarter of peak CapEx and projects a cash burn trajectory that justifies near-total exit. Neither of you has enough data to make those claims with the confidence you're displaying, and that's exactly why a measured approach — one that respects both the operational improvement and the cash burn without pretending either is a proven trend — is the right path.

Let me start with the aggressive analyst because the logical gaps are the most consequential. The Amazon comparison is the tell here, and the conservative analyst actually nailed this — but then fumbled the conclusion. Amazon in 2001 is not an argument for holding 30 to 50 percent through a drawdown. It's an argument for understanding that thesis correctness and position sizing are two completely different things. Amazon's thesis was correct. The people who held 50 percent of their portfolio through an 80 percent decline still got wiped out even though they were "right." The ones who survived and thrived were the ones who sized appropriately — trimmed into the decline, retained a position that could tolerate the drawdown without forcing them to sell at the bottom, and then added back when the thesis confirmed. The aggressive analyst is using Amazon to argue for retaining a large residual, but Amazon actually argues for the opposite: size the residual so that even a catastrophic drawdown doesn't take you out of the game. Fifty percent drawdown tolerance on 30 to 50 percent of the position is not that. On a standard portfolio, that's accepting that the residual could lose a third to a quarter of its value before the $107.01 catastrophic exit fires. That's not sizing for survival. That's sizing for conviction, which is exactly what got people in trouble on Amazon in 2001.

The cash fortress argument is where the aggressive analyst's case really breaks down. The $93.52 billion in cash is real. The investments it funds are real. The valuation model argument is where both analysts get themselves into trouble. The aggressive says the five-fold range in model outputs means the models can't price SPCX, so we should retain meaningful exposure because the asymmetric outcome is to the upside. The conservative makes the opposite error, treating the models as directional signals strong enough to justify near-total exit. But the conservative is right that all three models point down, and the aggressive is right that the models have enormous error bars. The synthesis is: the models provide a strong directional signal that the stock is overvalued, but the degree of overvaluation is highly uncertain. That argues for meaningful reduction in exposure, but not for near-total exit, because the uncertainty means the bearish case could be less severe than the models suggest. The conservative's 70 to 80 percent trim is justified by the directional signal. The aggressive's 50 to 70 percent trim is justified by the uncertainty. Now let me turn to the conservative analyst, because the stop placement issue is genuinely dangerous and the aggressive analyst is right to call it out. That keeps you in the game. This puts the effective forward PE at roughly 150-340x — well above the headline 85.53x. When a company's own bull-case math fails to justify the current price, the valuation signal is decisive.

The macro environment is specifically hostile to this asset class. The 86% prediction-market probability of zero Fed rate cuts in 2026 (increasing weekly with $7.44M in volume) means higher discount rates persist for the entire holding period of any residual position. Japan's recession probability surging to 33% signals global growth deterioration affecting aerospace supply chains. The 8% US recession probability is broadly supportive of the economy but irrelevant to the multiple — as the neutral analyst sharply observed, the macro is specifically hostile to high-multiple, long-duration growth names while being broadly supportive of the underlying business. This bifurcation justifies trimming for the multiple while retaining a small residual for the business.

Higher-timeframe technicals are bearish and deteriorating. The weekly SuperTrend is down at $192.99 (40% above current price — requires a massive rally to flip) and the monthly SuperTrend is down at $313.05 (56% gap). MACD momentum is decelerating (2.52→2.19→2.14), OBV deteriorated (-273M→-358M) during a 36.6% rally, and RSI ceilinged at 59 — structurally weak for a new uptrend. However, this is NOT a Sell because the operational improvement is real and broad-based. Revenue grew 92% YoY ($4.07B→$7.81B). Gross margins expanded 1,140 bps (43.9%→55.3%). EBIT swung from -$3.61B to +$111M. Operating cash flow went from -$376M to +$2.42B. These span four quarters across multiple metrics — it's a pattern, not a single anomalous data point, despite the conservative's attempt to dismiss it as "two data points."
